which commutes with the two blow-down maps.
Proof. The pullback by Φ−1 identifies the ideal sheaf of 𝐴 with the ideal sheaf of Φ( 𝐴) and therefore identifies
their Rees algebras. Taking relative Proj gives the asserted map. Applying the same construction to Φ−1
gives its inverse.                                                                                             □
   We state the precise external result needed for point centres. The word “tame” has the meaning fixed in the
definition preceding Theorem 4.7.
Theorem 5.6. Let 𝑛 > 1 and let 𝐷 ⊂ C𝑛 be a closed discrete set.
     (i) If 𝐷 is infinite and tame, meaning that an automorphism of C𝑛 carries it onto N × {0} ⊂ C × C𝑛−1 ,
         then Bl𝐷 C𝑛 is weakly subelliptic and hence Oka.
                  OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                           67


 (ii) If 𝐷 is finite, then Bl𝐷 C𝑛 is of Class 𝒜 and hence Oka.
   The infinite-centre statement is the tame discrete blow-up theorem [20, Proposition 6.4.12]; its Oka
conclusion is also Kusakabe’s [21, Proposition 4.10]. The finite-centre statement follows from Gromov’s
preservation of Class 𝒜 under finitely many point blow-ups, as recorded in [27, p. 152]. Tameness matters in
the infinite case: Kusakabe also gives closed discrete centres with non-Oka blow-ups.
Corollary 5.7. Let 𝑞 : C𝑛 → 𝑉 be a surjective unramified holomorphic covering, let 𝑆 ⊂ 𝑉 be closed and
discrete, and suppose that the complete inverse image 𝑞 −1 (𝑆) is tame. Then Bl𝑆 𝑉 is Oka.
Proof. Theorem 5.6 makes Bl𝑞 −1 (𝑆) C𝑛 Oka. By Lemma 5.4, this manifold covers Bl𝑆 𝑉 unramifiedly.
Covering invariance, Theorem 4.1, descends the Oka property.                                    □
Corollary 5.8. In Corollary 5.7, assume 𝑛 = 3. It is enough to find a surjective complex-linear projection
𝐿 : C3 → C2 such that 𝐿 (𝑞 −1 (𝑆)) is closed and discrete.
Proof. If 𝑞 −1 (𝑆) is infinite, the Rosay–Rudin criterion, Theorem 4.7, makes it tame. A finite set is tame by
definition. Apply Corollary 5.7.                                                                             □
Lemma 5.9. Let 𝑍 be a complex manifold and let 𝑆 ⊂ 𝑍 be closed and discrete. Assume:
  (i) 𝑍 \ 𝑆 is Oka;
 (ii) for every 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆 there is an analytic-Zariski-open neighbourhood 𝑉𝑠 ⊂ 𝑍 of 𝑠 such that Bl𝑆∩𝑉𝑠 𝑉𝑠 is Oka.
Then Bl𝑆 𝑍 is Oka.
Proof. Let 𝜋 : Bl𝑆 𝑍 → 𝑍 be the blow-down and set 𝐸 = 𝜋 −1 (𝑆). By Lemma 5.2,
                                               (Bl𝑆 𝑍) \ 𝐸 ≃ 𝑍 \ 𝑆
is analytic-Zariski open and Oka. For 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆, the inverse image 𝜋 −1 (𝑉𝑠 ) is canonically Bl𝑆∩𝑉𝑠 𝑉𝑠 ; it is
analytic-Zariski open, is Oka, and contains the complete exceptional fibre 𝜋 −1 (𝑠). These opens cover Bl𝑆 𝑍.
Kusakabe localization, Theorem 3.2, proves the claim.                                                      □
   The same statement remains true if 𝑉𝑠 is obtained by pulling back a principal open through a biholomorphism
of 𝑍: use Lemma 5.5 before applying Lemma 5.9.
5.2. Scalar and corrected incidence charts. We retain the notation of Subsection 4.3. Thus 𝑇 = (C∗ ) 2 ,
𝑔 ∈ C[𝑢 ±1 , 𝑣 ±1 ], and
                                  𝑆 𝑔 = {(𝑞, 𝐴, 𝐵) ∈ 𝑇 × C2 : 𝐴𝐵 = 𝑔(𝑞)}reg .

                                                   𝑆 = 𝐷 ∪ 𝐹.
Theorem 4.21 says that 𝑆 𝑔 \ 𝑆 is Oka. We now treat the points above 𝑆 in the blow-up.

                                         Bl𝑆∩𝑆 𝐴 𝑆 𝐴,   Bl𝑆∩𝑆𝐵 𝑆 𝐵 ,

    Suppose (𝑚, 𝜎) remains in a compact subset of C2 . Then 𝑀 = 𝑒 𝑚 and 𝐴 = 𝑒 𝜎 lie in compact annuli.
Properness of 𝑀 on the normalizations of the finitely many carrier curves confines the base point to a compact
part of those curves. On 𝐶𝜈 the equation is 𝐴𝐵 = 𝑐 𝜈 , so 𝐵 = 𝑐 𝜈 /𝐴 also lies in a compact annulus. The points
of the closed discrete set 𝐷 under consideration therefore lie in a fixed compact subset of 𝑆 𝐴 and are finite in
68                          YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


number. For each such point the possible projected logarithms form a product of two arithmetic progressions,
which meets a compact set in finitely many points. The lift of the finite set 𝐹 is a finite union of the same type
of product lattices. Hence the complete projected image is closed and discrete.
   Corollary 5.8 now applies to 𝜋 𝐴 : C3 → 𝑆 𝐴 and proves that Bl𝑆∩𝑆 𝐴 𝑆 𝐴 is Oka.                               □

  The residual axis of 𝑆 𝑔 is the set of smooth points with 𝐴 = 𝐵 = 0. Carrier points never lie there because
𝐴𝐵 = 𝑐 𝜈 ≠ 0, so an element of the centre on the axis necessarily belongs to the finite set 𝐹.

                           𝐴(Φ𝑡 (𝑒)) =                 𝑝(𝑒)
                                        𝑡 (𝑊 𝑓 ) (𝑒),
                                       
                                                                              𝑝(𝑒) = 0.
                                       
The first order-four function is not identically zero because its derivative at 𝑡 = 0 is (𝑊 𝑓 ) (𝑒) ≠ 0. Thus in
both orders the bad times for which the displayed value vanishes form a discrete subset of C.
70                         YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


   Since 𝑚(𝑒) ∉ 𝑆 𝐿 , we may adjoin ( 𝑝(𝑒), 𝑚(𝑒)) to 𝐵+◦ and prescribe a time outside this bad set. If that
projected value already lies in 𝐵+◦ , the exponential-polynomial estimate used in the simultaneous-escape
lemma holds on a sufficiently far tail of the chosen real ray. Removing the discrete bad times still leaves a time
on that tail, so the same value both moves 𝑒 and sends the complete carrier fibre beyond its escape threshold.
Cartan interpolation on the resulting closed discrete set, with prescribed values divided by the nonzero factors
𝛾(𝑚), determines 𝐺. The resulting wrong-line-fixing replica moves 𝑒 into the 𝐴-principal open.
   We spell out the missing case in the local-finiteness argument. On the large end with 𝑚 ∉ 𝑆 𝐿 , the escape
threshold leaves only finitely many points of 𝐵+◦ . On the large end with 𝑚 ∈ 𝑆 𝐿 , the replica is the identity. If
(𝜎, 𝑚) remains compact, then 𝐴 = 𝑒 𝜎 lies in a compact annulus and properness of the character confines the
carrier base to a compact set. The nonzero carrier factor is bounded there, so 𝑝 = 𝑓 /𝐴 is bounded; together
with | 𝑝| ≥ 1, this confines the corresponding intermediate points to a compact subset. Closed discreteness
leaves only finitely many of them. On the small end the Taylor difference quotient gives 𝐴′ − 𝐴 = 𝑂 (1),
whereas the nonzero carrier factor gives | 𝐴| → ∞ if 𝑝 → 0; hence 𝑝 is bounded away from zero and the same
compactness argument applies. These are exactly the three cases in Proposition 4.36 and Proposition 4.49.
   The finite part produces only finitely many intermediate lifts for bounded 𝑚, and the remaining kernel
branches change only the unused coordinate 𝑛. In order four this assertion after the replica is precisely
Lemma 4.43(c). Thus the complete further lift has closed discrete (𝜎, 𝑚)-projection.                             □

Corollary 5.19. For every 𝑎 ∈ A and 𝑝 ∈ 𝑋𝑎 , the blow-up Bl 𝑝 𝑋𝑎 is an Oka complex manifold diffeomorphic
to P3 , but it is not biholomorphic to the standard projective threefold.
Proof. The Oka assertion is the one-point case of Theorem 1.2. Since 𝑋𝑎 is diffeomorphic to 𝑆 6 , the
smooth point-blow-up is diffeomorphic to P3 ; this standard identification is recalled by Huckleberry–
Kebekus–Peternell [4]. Let 𝐸 ≃ P2 be its exceptional divisor. The blow-up canonical bundle formula
gives
                                  𝐾Bl 𝑝 𝑋𝑎 ≃ 𝜋 ∗ 𝐾 𝑋𝑎 ⊗ OBl 𝑝 𝑋𝑎 (2𝐸).
Now 𝐻 2 (𝑋𝑎 , Z) = 0 by Alpöge’s smooth-type theorem [2, Theorem 8.1], and [𝐸] generates 𝐻 2 (Bl 𝑝 𝑋𝑎 , Z) ≃
Z: its restriction to 𝐸 is 𝑐 1 (O𝐸 (−1)), a generator of 𝐻 2 (𝐸, Z). Consequently 𝑐 1 (𝑇Bl 𝑝 𝑋𝑎 ) = −2[𝐸] has
divisibility 2. For the standard P3 , 𝑐 1 (𝑇P3 ) = 4ℎ with ℎ primitive. A biholomorphism preserves the first
Chern class and its divisibility, so the two complex structures differ.                                    □
  The point-centre theorem concerns simultaneous blow-ups at distinct points. For an arbitrary smooth
compact curve, the available general conclusion is the Oka–1 property. We record it in the same section
because it clarifies exactly which flexibility survives without the discrete-centre arguments above.
Corollary 5.20. If 𝐶 ⊂ 𝑋𝑎 is a smooth compact complex curve, possibly disconnected, then Bl𝐶 𝑋𝑎 is Oka–1.
The same holds after any finite sequence of blow-ups along smooth centres.
Proof. Every Oka manifold is Oka–1. The blow-up invariance theorem of Forstnerič–Lárusson [28,
Theorem 3.1] applies to each smooth centre, including disconnected ones. Iterate it for the last assertion. □
   For the standard exceptional curve in the local conifold model, the blow-up has the stronger algebraic
ellipticity property.
Proposition 5.21. Let 𝑅 = Tot(OP1 (−1) ⊕ OP1 (−1)) and let 𝐶 ≃ P1 be its zero section. Then
                                        Bl𝐶 𝑅 ≃ Tot(OP1 ×P1 (−1, −1)),
and this smooth algebraic threefold is algebraically elliptic and Oka.
Proof. For a vector bundle 𝐸 → 𝐵, the incidence description of the blow-up of its zero section identifies
Bl 𝐵 𝐸 with the tautological line bundle on P(𝐸). Here 𝐸 = OP1 (−1) ⊗ C2 , so P(𝐸) = P1 × P1 and its
tautological line is O (−1, −1). The four products of standard affine charts on the two projective-line factors
trivialize this line bundle; their inverse images are copies of A3 . A smooth algebraic threefold with such
a Zariski-open cover lies in class 𝒜0 , hence in class 𝒜 in the terminology of Lárusson–Truong. Their
class-𝒜 theorem [27, Corollary 2] makes it algebraically subelliptic; Kaliman–Zaidenberg’s equivalence [29,
Theorem 1.1] makes it algebraically elliptic. The Oka assertion follows from Gromov’s theorem [1].           □

                              6. Logarithmic geometry and deformations
  Fix an admissible parameter 𝑎 ∈ A, and abbreviate (𝑋, 𝑓 , 𝑊) = (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 , 𝑊𝑎 ). The finite uniformizations,
period equivariance, cusp quotient, and gluing used in this and the next section were established in Section 2.
No topological hypothesis on 𝑋 is used.
74                         YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


6.1. The root base and cusp resolution. Set 𝑚 1 = 3 and 𝑚 2 = 4, and let 𝜎 𝑗 denote the canonical section of
O𝐵 ( 𝑝 𝑗 ). We use the analytic root stack
                               √︁                    √︁
                          B = 3 (O𝐵 ( 𝑝 1 ), 𝜎1 ) × 𝐵 4 (O𝐵 ( 𝑝 2 ), 𝜎2 ), 𝜌 : B −→ 𝐵.                 (6.1)
Thus an object of the 𝑗-th factor over a complex space 𝑇 → 𝐵 is a line bundle ℛ on 𝑇, a section 𝑟, and an
isomorphism ℛ⊗𝑚 𝑗 ≃ O𝐵 ( 𝑝 𝑗 )| 𝑇 carrying 𝑟 𝑚 𝑗 to 𝜎 𝑗 | 𝑇 . We write (ℛ 𝑗 , 𝜏 𝑗 ) for the universal root on B.
Lemma 6.1. There is an isomorphism of smooth proper Deligne–Mumford curves
                       B ≃ Pstk (3, 4) := [(C2 \ {0})/C∗ ],             𝜆 · (𝑥, 𝑦) = (𝜆3 𝑥, 𝜆4 𝑦),

                             (ℛ1 , 𝜏1 ) = (O B (4), 𝑦),        (ℛ2 , 𝜏2 ) = (O B (3), 𝑥).
Moreover,
                      Pic(B) ≃ Z,    O B (1) is a generator,      𝜌 ∗ O𝐵 (1) = O B (12),                      (6.2)

If 𝑆 = C[𝑥, 𝑦] with deg 𝑥 = 3 and deg 𝑦 = 4, then, for every 𝑟 ∈ Z,
                              𝐻 0 (B, O B (𝑟)) = 𝑆𝑟 ,        𝐻 1 (B, O B (𝑟)) = 𝑆 ∨−𝑟 −7 .                    (6.4)
The higher coherent cohomology vanishes. In particular, O (−1), O (−2), O (−5), and O (−6) are acyclic,
and 𝐻 1 (B, O (1)) = 0.
Proof. The stabilizer along 𝑦 = 0 is 𝜇3 , and the stabilizer along 𝑥 = 0 is 𝜇4 . The two displayed equivariant
functions therefore provide the universal roots: O (4) ⊗3 = O (12) and O (3) ⊗4 = O (12). The complement
of the origin in C2 has trivial algebraic Picard group, since the removed set has codimension two in the
factorial affine plane. Its invertible regular functions are constants. A C∗ -linearization of the trivial bundle is
therefore a character, indexed by Z; the corresponding bundles are precisely O (𝑟). This proves the Picard
assertion directly. Analytification induces an equivalence on coherent sheaves for this proper algebraic stack
[12, Theorem 7.4]; in particular every analytic line bundle is algebraizable and the same Picard calculation
holds for the analytic stack B. The degree-12 invariant linear system gives the coarse map and hence (6.2).
The canonical character of the quotient is the negative sum of the weights, so 𝐾 B = O (−3 − 4). The point
𝑝 0 is nonstacky and has class O (12), which gives 𝑇B (−𝑝 0 ) = O (7 − 12). For cohomology, cover B by the
quotient charts 𝑥 ≠ 0 and 𝑦 ≠ 0. Each is a quotient of an affine line by 𝜇3 or 𝜇4 ; since these groups are
linearly reductive over C, the charts and their intersection have no higher coherent cohomology. In degree 𝑟
the resulting Čech complex is
                                           𝑆[𝑥 −1 ] 𝑟 ⊕ 𝑆[𝑦 −1 ] 𝑟 −→ 𝑆[𝑥 −1 , 𝑦 −1 ] 𝑟 .
Its kernel is 𝑆𝑟 , and its cokernel has basis the monomials 𝑥 −𝑖 𝑦 − 𝑗 with 𝑖, 𝑗 ≥ 1 and 3𝑖 + 4 𝑗 = −𝑟. Pairing
𝑥 −𝑖 𝑦 − 𝑗 with 𝑥 𝑖−1 𝑦 𝑗 −1 identifies this cokernel with 𝑆 ∨−𝑟 −7 and proves (6.4). There is no higher cohomology
because this is a two-chart acyclic cover. The asserted vanishings follow because none of the relevant graded
pieces contains a monomial.                                                                                       □

   Analytic root stacks and their quotient charts are supplied by [10, Definition 4.1, Proposition 4.2, and §5];
the line-bundle-and-section universal property and its 2-Cartesian base-change form are [11, Definition 2.2.1
and Remarks 2.2.2–2.2.3]. In the argument below, representability, properness, and flatness are checked
directly after pullback to the displayed analytic quotient charts, rather than being delegated to a blanket locality
theorem. The direct two-chart calculation above proves the weighted quotient cohomology without invoking
a separate duality result. The coherent-sheaf equivalence of [12, Theorem 7.4] transfers this calculation and
the algebraic Picard group to the analytic stack used here.

  It remains to check the multiplication, since the dimensions alone do not exclude the zero pairing.
The ordered normalization complex carries the Alexander–Whitney product. If 𝛼 = (𝛼ℎ , 𝛼𝑣 , 𝛼𝑑 ) and

                                          [𝛼] ⌣ [𝛽] = 𝛼ℎ 𝛽 𝑑 − 𝛼𝑑 𝛽 ℎ .                                   (6.15)
For 𝛼 = (1, 1, 0) and 𝛽 = (0, 1, 1), the right-hand side is 1. Since both sides of (6.14) are one-dimensional,
the map is an isomorphism.                                                                                     □
   The multiplicative comparison used here is internal to the displayed resolution. The unit map from O𝑊
to the normalized semisimplicial complex is a multiplicative quasi-isomorphism by Lemma 6.4. Endow
its normalized cochains with the Alexander–Whitney product. Applying derived global sections to this
differential graded algebra computes both the ordinary derived product on 𝑅Γ(𝑊, O𝑊 ) and the two-triangle
formula above. Thus (6.15) is the sheaf-cohomology cup product, not merely the product of the cellular dual
complex.

   The proof uses the following pointwise form of analytic base change and the top-degree form of relative
duality. Both are statements for complex spaces on an analytic étale atlas; their canonical maps descend along
the atlas groupoid.
Lemma 6.8. Let 𝑆 be a smooth analytic Deligne–Mumford curve with finite inertia over C, and let 𝑝 : 𝑌 → 𝑆
be a representable proper flat holomorphic map. Assume that O𝑌 is flat over 𝑆 and that, for a fixed 𝑞, the
number ℎ𝑞 (𝑌𝑠 , O𝑌𝑠 ) is constant on geometric points of 𝑆. Then 𝑅 𝑞 𝑝 ∗ O𝑌 is locally free and the canonical map
                                                            ∼
                                      (𝑅 𝑞 𝑝 ∗ O𝑌 ) ⊗ 𝑘 (𝑠) −
                                                            → 𝐻 𝑞 (𝑌𝑠 , O𝑌𝑠 )                             (6.20)
is an isomorphism for every geometric point 𝑠. These isomorphisms are canonical on étale atlases, compatible
on their double overlaps, and hence descend to 𝑆.
Proof. Choose an analytic étale atlas 𝑈 → 𝑆. The base change 𝑝𝑈 : 𝑌 ×𝑆 𝑈 → 𝑈 is a proper map of
complex spaces, 𝑈 is reduced, and O𝑌 ×𝑆 𝑈 is 𝑈-flat. The constancy hypothesis therefore allows one to apply
[13, Chapter III, Theorem 4.12(ii), pp. 134–135]; local freeness and pointwise comparison follow. The
arbitrary analytic base-change compatibility in [13, Chapter III, Theorem 4.10, pp. 132–133] identifies the
two pullbacks on 𝑈 ×𝑆 𝑈. The cocycle condition follows from functoriality of the canonical comparison
maps, so the vector bundle and its fibre comparisons descend along the analytic groupoid. A geometric point
may be lifted to the atlas, where the asserted map is exactly the complex-space comparison just cited.    □
Lemma 6.9. Let 𝑝 : 𝑌 → 𝑆 be a representable proper flat Gorenstein holomorphic map of relative dimension
two between smooth analytic Deligne–Mumford stacks. Assume that 𝑝 ∗ O𝑌 = O𝑆 and that the relevant direct
images are locally free. Then the trace pairing induces an isomorphism
                                                          ∼
                                           (𝑅 2 𝑝 ∗ O𝑌 ) ∨ −
                                                           → 𝑝 ∗ 𝜔𝑌 /𝑆 ,                                  (6.21)
compatible with étale pullback and descent.
                   OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                             79


Proof. Again choose an analytic étale atlas 𝑈 → 𝑆 and write 𝑝𝑈 : 𝑌𝑈 → 𝑈. Relative analytic duality gives
the canonical isomorphism
                                 𝑅 𝑝𝑈∗ 𝜔𝑌𝑈 /𝑈 [2] ≃ 𝑅H 𝑜𝑚𝑈 (𝑅 𝑝𝑈∗ O𝑌𝑈 , O𝑈 ).                                (6.22)
The relative trace and duality morphism for proper analytic maps are constructed in [14, p. 261 and pp. 264–
266]; the identification of the dualizing complex of a smooth analytic space, and the invertible dualizing sheaf
in the Gorenstein case, are [15, §3, Theorem 1, p. 81, and Proposition 2, p. 91]. Cancelling the pullback of
𝜔𝑈 in that duality gives (6.22).
   Because the direct images of O𝑌𝑈 are locally free, the hyper-Ext spectral sequence has no positive Ext
terms. Taking the degree corresponding to top fibre cohomology gives (𝑅 2 𝑝𝑈∗ O𝑌𝑈 ) ∨ ≃ 𝑝𝑈∗ 𝜔𝑌𝑈 /𝑈 . On
𝑈 ×𝑆 𝑈, the two pullbacks agree: the projections are local biholomorphisms, and the relative trace, projection
formula, and duality morphism are canonical and local. They therefore satisfy the groupoid cocycle condition
and descend to the displayed isomorphism on 𝑆.                                                                □
   Set
                                                  𝐺 = 𝑅 1 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 .
Proposition 6.10. The sheaves 𝑅 𝑞 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 , for 𝑞 = 0, 1, 2, are locally free of ranks 1, 2, 1 and satisfy geometric-
point base change. They are
                 𝑅 0 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 = O B ,    𝑅 1 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 ≃ O B ⊕ O B (−1),         𝑅 2 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 ≃ O B (−1).           (6.23)
The indicated direct-sum splitting of 𝑅 1 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 need not be canonical; its [𝛾]-subline is fixed.
Proof. The geometric fibres over the smooth locus are complex two-tori. The fibres over the two stacky points
become smooth complex two-tori on the finite root charts. In all these cases their structure-sheaf cohomology
has dimensions (1, 2, 1). The cusp fibre has the same dimensions by (6.13). The hypotheses of Lemma 6.8
are supplied by Lemma 6.3; in particular, flatness of 𝑓¯ makes O𝑋 flat over B. Thus the direct images are
locally free with the asserted ranks and satisfy (6.20). All fibres are connected, so the unit map identifies
𝑅 0 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 with O B .
   On an etale atlas of B, the source is smooth and each fibre is locally a Cartier divisor in the smooth total
space; hence 𝑓¯ is Gorenstein of relative dimension two. Apply Lemma 6.9, then use (6.19) and the projection
formula:
                                (𝑅 2 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 ) ∨ ≃ 𝑓¯∗ 𝜔 𝑋/B
                                              ≃ 𝑓¯∗ 𝑓¯∗ (det 𝐸) −1
                                              ≃ (det 𝐸) −1 ⊗ 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 ≃ (det 𝐸) −1 .
Dualizing and using (6.16) proves the 𝑅 2 formula in (6.23). It remains to identify 𝐺 = 𝑅 1 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 . Cup product
defines a morphism of line bundles
                                             Û2
                                                𝐺 −→ 𝑅 2 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 .                                         (6.24)
By geometric-point base change, this is the usual nondegenerate cup product on every smooth torus fibre. At
the cusp it is the isomorphism (6.14); at a finite stacky point the same statement follows after pulling back to
its smooth root chart. Thus (6.24) is an isomorphism on every geometric point and therefore
                                         det 𝐺 ≃ 𝑅 2 𝑓¯∗ O𝑋 ≃ O B (−1).                                   (6.25)
   We next construct a nowhere-zero section of 𝐺 without appealing to the Kodaira–Spencer class. Recall the
marked lattice
                                             Λ = Z⟨b 𝛾, b
                                                        𝑢, 𝑤
                                                           b, b
                                                              𝛿⟩
and its first coordinate character 𝛾 ∈ Hom(Λ, Z). The finite identities (2.21) give 𝛾 ◦ 𝐴 𝑗 = 𝛾. On a smooth
fibre 𝐹 = C2 /Ω𝑎 Λ, constant group cocycles give a natural map from the right-hand side below to 𝐻 1 (𝐹, O𝐹 ):
                                    𝐻 1 (𝐹, O𝐹 ) ≃ (Λ∗ ⊗ C)/rowspanC Ω𝑎 .                                    (6.26)
Indeed, a linear function ℓ has coboundary 𝜆 ↦→ ℓ(Ω𝑎 𝜆). Conversely, if an entire function has constant
differences under all period translations, its derivatives are periodic and descend to holomorphic functions
on the compact torus; they are therefore constant, so the function is affine-linear. This proves precisely that
the constant-cocycle map has kernel rowspanC Ω𝑎 . Its quotient has dimension two, and it injects into the
two-dimensional space 𝐻 1 (𝐹, O𝐹 ); hence it is an isomorphism and proves (6.26).

6.3. Tangent cohomology from logarithmic to ordinary fields. The projection formula, together with
(6.16) and (6.23), gives
                               𝑅 0 𝑓¯∗ V = O ⊕ O (−1),

and
                                            𝑅 0 𝑓¯∗ H = O (−5),
                                            𝑅 1 𝑓¯∗ H = O (−5) ⊕ O (−6),
                                            𝑅 2 𝑓¯∗ H = O (−6).                                              (6.30)
All line bundles in these displays except the displayed trivial summands are acyclic by (6.4). The weighted
quotient calculation also gives vanishing in coherent degrees above one. Hence the Leray spectral sequence
has only columns 𝑝 = 0, 1 and no possible nonzero higher differential. In degree zero, the edge morphism
gives 𝐻 0 (𝑋, F ) = 𝐻 0 (B, 𝑅 0 𝑓¯∗ F ). For F = V, H and 𝑛 ≥ 1, it yields
                     0 −→ 𝐻 1 (B, 𝑅 𝑛−1 𝑓¯∗ F ) −→ 𝐻 𝑛 (𝑋, F ) −→ 𝐻 0 (B, 𝑅 𝑛 𝑓¯∗ F ) −→ 0.
Consequently,
                             𝐻 • (𝑋, V) = (C, C, 0, 0),     𝐻 • (𝑋, H ) = (0, 0, 0, 0).                      (6.31)

   On the dense cusp torus consider

In a maximal toric chart the three ray generators form a lattice basis and 𝑞 = 𝑧0 𝑧 1 𝑧 2 . The associated character
matrix has determinant ±1, so Θ = ±𝑑𝑧0 ∧ 𝑑𝑧 1 ∧ 𝑑𝑧 2 . Thus Θ extends holomorphically and without zeros
across every toric boundary stratum. Under the full cusp action, 𝑑 log 𝑥 𝑖 changes only by a multiple of 𝑑𝑞 or
𝑑 log 𝑞; wedging with 𝑑𝑞 removes every added term. Hence Θ is invariant under all 𝜆 ∈ Λ and descends to a
nowhere-zero section of 𝐾 𝑋 on a neighbourhood of 𝑊.
   The adjunction formula for the immersion 𝜈 : 𝐸 0 → 𝑋 gives

   The hexagonal toric surface 𝐸 0 is smooth, complete, and rational. Serre duality therefore gives

Lemma 6.14. The formulae (6.43), (6.44), (6.45), and (6.48) determine a 1-cocycle on the chosen analytic
presentation of 𝑋𝑎 . Its canonical descent, or Čech, image is a class in 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , V𝑎 ), and its image in
𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑇𝑋𝑎 ) is the Kodaira–Spencer class of the actual 𝑎-family.
Proof. The core semidirect relation, both finite cyclic relations, the two finite-connector conjugacies, and the
cusp equivariance were checked above on a generating set of arrows. Equation (6.42) then gives the cocycle
relation for every composite arrow. Descent along the presentation gives the asserted sheaf-cohomology class.
The representatives have no base component; at the cusp, the displayed toric expression for 𝑉0 is logarithmic
along 𝑞 −1 (0). Hence the cocycle is vertical and logarithmic, so it takes values in the intrinsic kernel V𝑎 .
Differentiating the transition arrows of the parameter family gives the connecting class of
                                      0 −→ 𝑇𝑋𝑎 −→ 𝑇𝔛Δ | 𝑋𝑎 −→ O𝑋𝑎 −→ 0.
By the derivative convention (6.41), the image of our cocycle in the absolute tangent sheaf is exactly this
Kodaira–Spencer class.                                                                                   □
   The restriction of the scalar cocycle 𝜂 to the lattice arrows is the character 𝛾. Consequently, on a smooth
fibre the restriction is
                                   u2 ⊗ [𝛾] ∈ 𝐻 0 (𝐹𝑧 , 𝑇𝐹𝑧 ) ⊗ 𝐻 1 (𝐹𝑧 , O𝐹𝑧 ).                         (6.50)
The nonvanishing of [𝛾] on smooth fibres and at the cusp was proved in Proposition 6.10, using (6.26) and
(6.28).
   We now make the global bridge explicit. Projection formula and (6.39) give
                   𝑅 0 𝑓¯𝑎∗ V𝑎 ≃ 𝐸,       𝑅 1 𝑓¯𝑎∗ V𝑎 ≃ 𝐸 ⊗ 𝐺 ≃ O B ⊕ 2O B (−1) ⊕ O B (−2).
The root-stack line-bundle calculation gives 𝐻 1 (B, 𝐸) = 𝐻 2 (B, 𝐸) = 0 and no global sections of the three
negative summands. Here the 𝐻 2 -vanishing is the coherent cohomological-dimension-one statement for the
tame root-stack curve proved in the preceding calculation. The five-term sequence for Leray consequently
reduces to an isomorphism
                                                      ∼
                              edge : 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , V𝑎 ) −−→ 𝐻 0 B, 𝑅 1 𝑓¯𝑎∗ V𝑎 ≃ C.
                                                                          
                                                                                                      (6.51)
The edge image of the cocycle in Lemma 6.14 is computed fibrewise by (6.50). Geometric-point base change
identifies its restriction over the smooth locus with u2 ⊗ [𝛾]. Since 𝑅 1 𝑓¯𝑎∗ V𝑎 is locally free, two sections
agreeing on this dense open set agree globally. The edge image is therefore precisely the section u2 ⊗ [𝛾] of
the unique trivial summand in 𝐸 ⊗ 𝐺. Thus the global cocycle generates 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , V𝑎 ).
Proposition 6.15. The actual family has an isomorphism
                                                      ∼
                                         KS𝑎 : 𝑇𝑎 A −−→ 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑇𝑋𝑎 ).
Proof. The tangent vector 𝜕/𝜕𝑎 is represented by the cocycle of Lemma 6.14. Its edge image is the nonzero
generator u2 ⊗ [𝛾]. The two maps in (6.40) carry this generator to a nonzero element of the one-dimensional
space 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑇𝑋𝑎 ). Since 𝑇𝑎 A is also one-dimensional, the Kodaira–Spencer map is an isomorphism. □
   The nonzero Kodaira–Spencer class now determines the analytic deformation germ. We use the following
form of the Kuranishi theorem. For a compact complex manifold 𝑋, the analytic Kuranishi theorem provides
a closed, possibly nonreduced, analytic subgerm
                                             𝐾 ↩−→ (𝐻 1 (𝑋, 𝑇𝑋 ), 0)
and a complete deformation over 𝐾 such that the Kuranishi Kodaira–Spencer map identifies 𝑇0 𝐾 with
𝐻 1 (𝑋, 𝑇𝑋 ). Equivalently, the defining obstruction map has zero differential at the origin. Every sufficiently
                   OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                                  85


small pointed analytic deformation of 𝑋 admits, after shrinking, a pointed holomorphic classifying map 𝜙 to
𝐾, and its Kodaira–Spencer map satisfies
                                                 KSgiven = KS𝐾 ◦𝑑𝜙0 ,                                             (6.52)
up to the invertible linear action induced by changing the marking of the central fibre. No uniqueness of 𝜙 is
used.
   We use this theorem in the analytic-space sense, including possibly nonreduced bases, as formulated by
Douady [16, pp. 7, 10–12]. Equation (6.52) is the standard naturality of the connecting homomorphism
defining the Kodaira–Spencer class.
Proposition 6.16. The Kuranishi germ of 𝑋𝑎 is smooth and reduced of dimension one:
                                                  Kur(𝑋𝑎 ) ≃ (C, 0).
After shrinking and a local reparameterization, the actual family is miniversal at 𝑎.
Proof. Set 𝐾 = Kur(𝑋𝑎 ). By Theorem 6.12, the ambient space 𝐻 1 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑇𝑋𝑎 ) is one-dimensional. Choose its
coordinate 𝑡 and write
                                           O𝐾 ,0 = C{𝑡}/𝐼.                                         (6.53)
Let 𝑠 = 𝑎 ′ − 𝑎 be the coordinate on a sufficiently small disc in the actual parameter family. Completeness
supplies a classifying map
                                              𝜙 : (C, 0) 𝑠 −→ 𝐾.
After composing with the closed embedding of 𝐾 into the 𝑡-line, the map on local rings is
                                          C{𝑡} −→ C{𝑠},               𝑡 ↦−→ 𝑔(𝑠).                                 (6.54)
By (6.52) and Proposition 6.15, one has 𝑔 ′ (0) ≠ 0. The one-dimensional holomorphic inverse function
theorem therefore makes (6.54) an isomorphism after shrinking; in particular, it is injective. Since the same
map factors through (6.53),
                                                            
                                      𝐼 ⊂ ker C{𝑡} −→ C{𝑠} = 0.
Thus 𝐼 = 0, including its nilpotent elements. Hence 𝐾 is a smooth reduced line, and 𝜙 is locally biholomorphic.
Pulling the Kuranishi family back by this biholomorphism proves miniversality of the actual family. This
argument proves neither uniqueness of classifying maps nor universality.                                     □
  The proof of Theorem 6.12 already identifies 𝐻 0 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑇𝑋𝑎 ) = C𝑉, where 𝑉 is the vertical field in (6.37).
We now integrate this field and determine the kernel of its action.
  The field 𝑉 has the global flow
                                        𝑇𝑐 [𝑧, 𝜁] = [𝑧, 𝜁 + 𝑐u2 ],            𝑐 ∈ C.                              (6.55)
It commutes with the core and finite arrows by 𝑅𝑔 u2 = u2 . At the cusp it becomes
                                           (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑞) ↦−→ (𝑥 1 , e2 𝜋i𝑐 𝑥 2 , 𝑞),                              (6.56)
which extends over the toric boundary and commutes with the cusp shears. Thus (6.55) defines a global
C-action.
Lemma 6.17. The kernel of 𝑐 ↦→ 𝑇𝑐 is Z.
Proof. The restriction of 𝑇𝑐 to the central fibre lifts through the normalization 𝜈 𝑎 : 𝐸 0 → 𝑊𝑎 in (2.48). On
the dense toric orbit of 𝐸 0 , the lift is multiplication of the second coordinate by e2 𝜋i𝑐 , as in (6.56). If 𝑇𝑐 is the
identity, uniqueness of the normalization lift therefore gives e2 𝜋i𝑐 = 1, so 𝑐 ∈ Z. Conversely, (6.38) gives
𝑛u2 = Ω𝑎 (𝑛b 𝛿) for every 𝑛 ∈ Z; hence 𝑇𝑛 is the identity on the core and, by continuation, on 𝑋𝑎 .                     □
   We use the following compact-complex automorphism theorem in exactly this form: if 𝑋 is a compact
complex manifold, then Aut(𝑋) has a natural finite-dimensional complex Lie group structure, and its Lie
algebra is canonically 𝐻 0 (𝑋, 𝑇𝑋 ).
   This is [17, Chapter III, §1, Theorem 1.1, p. 77].
Proposition 6.18. There is an isomorphism of complex Lie groups
                                                    Aut0 (𝑋𝑎 ) ≃ C∗ .
86                          YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


Proof. By Lemma 6.17, the flow gives an effective homomorphism
                                               C/Z ≃ C∗ −→ Aut0 (𝑋𝑎 ).
Its differential is the nonzero vector field 𝑉. By the 𝐻 0 calculation in the proof of Theorem 6.12 and the
compact-complex automorphism theorem, the connected target is one-dimensional and the differential is
a Lie algebra isomorphism. The holomorphic inverse function theorem shows that the image contains a
neighbourhood of the identity, hence is an open subgroup. An open subgroup of a connected topological
group is the whole group, and effectiveness gives injectivity. This proves the claimed isomorphism.       □
   The transformations (6.55) are defined on the relative total space and cover the identity on the explicit
𝑎-base. After choosing the local identification of that base with the Kuranishi germ from Proposition 6.16,
this constructed equivariant structure transports to one covering the identity of the Kuranishi base. We make
no claim that miniversality alone produces a canonical action of the full stabilizer.
Proof of Theorem 1.3. The Kodaira–Spencer assertion is Proposition 6.15. The smooth, reduced one-
dimensional Kuranishi germ and miniversality are Proposition 6.16. The vector-field statement follows from
the proof of Theorem 6.12, and Proposition 6.18 identifies the identity component. These arguments do not
establish universality and do not determine the possibly disconnected group Aut(𝑋𝑎 ); the latter is determined
by the global cusp argument in Subsection 7.3.                                                               □

   Equation (7.4) identifies the scheme-theoretic fibre over 𝑏 with the one over 𝜑(𝑏). An automorphism
of the base changes a local parameter only by a unit, so it preserves the multiplicity of the corresponding
Cartier fibre. By the fibre descriptions in Theorem 2.24, 𝑝 0 is uniquely characterized by having a fibre
with nonnormal reduced support. The remaining exceptional fibres are 3𝑆1 and 4𝑆2 , and their unequal
                    OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                              87


multiplicities distinguish 𝑝 1 from 𝑝 2 . Thus 𝜑 fixes 𝑝 0 , 𝑝 1 , 𝑝 2 . An automorphism of P1 fixing three distinct
points is the identity, and hence

Theorem 7.2. For every 𝑎 ∈ A, every holomorphic map ℎ : 𝑋𝑎 → P1 factors uniquely as ℎ = 𝜑 ◦ 𝑓 𝑎 with a
holomorphic map 𝜑 : P1 → P1 .
Proof. Fix 𝑎 and let 𝐹 be a smooth torus fibre of 𝑓 𝑎 . Since 𝑋𝑎 is diffeomorphic to 𝑆 6 , 𝐻 2 (𝑋𝑎 , Z) = 0.
Thus 𝑐 1 ((ℎ| 𝐹 ) ∗ OP1 (1)) = 0. If ℎ| 𝐹 were nonconstant, the pullback of the Fubini–Study form would be
semipositive and nonzero on an open subset of the Kähler surface 𝐹. Its wedge with a Kähler form on 𝐹
would have strictly positive integral, contradicting the vanishing of that Chern class. Hence ℎ is constant on
every smooth fibre.
    The proper holomorphic map ( 𝑓 𝑎 , ℎ) : 𝑋𝑎 → P1 × P1 has, by Remmert’s theorem, an irreducible compact
analytic image Γ of dimension one. Over the complement of the three special values, Γ is the graph of a
holomorphic map. The first projection 𝑝 : Γ → P1 is therefore generically one-to-one. It is also finite: a
positive-dimensional fibre of a map from an irreducible compact analytic curve would be the whole curve,
whereas 𝑝 is surjective. Thus 𝑝 is finite and bimeromorphic. On a coordinate disc of P1 , the finite algebra
𝑝 ∗ OΓ sits in the meromorphic function field of the disc and is integral over its holomorphic functions.
Normality of the disc forces equality, so 𝑝 is an isomorphism. The second projection gives 𝜑 and ℎ = 𝜑 ◦ 𝑓 𝑎 .
Surjectivity of 𝑓 𝑎 gives uniqueness.                                                                        □

                e 𝑗 , and the base change fixes 𝑠 𝑗 . On 𝑍 𝑗,𝑎 the transformation (𝑛, 𝑠 𝑗 ) ↦→ (𝑛, e−2 𝜋i/𝑚 𝑗 𝑠 𝑗 ) lifts to
the unique lift Φ
𝛼 𝑗,𝑎 . Both sides of (7.5) lift its conjugacy by the base-changed pair map, so uniqueness proves the equality.
Equivalently, on the punctured root disc a relation with 𝛼𝑟𝑗,𝑎′ would give e−2 𝜋i/𝑚 𝑗 𝑠 𝑗 = e−2 𝜋i𝑟/𝑚 𝑗 𝑠 𝑗 , hence
𝑟 ≡ 1 (mod 𝑚 𝑗 ).
   Finally, the induced integral 𝐻1 -matrix is locally constant on the connected root disc. The finite-to-core
gluing is a fibre translation and therefore acts trivially on 𝐻1 . The central and core matrices are consequently
equal.                                                                                                                    □

Proposition 7.8. For 𝑎 ∈ A and 𝑘 ∈ 2Z, translation by (7.21), its two finite corrections (7.27), and the cusp
multiplication (7.37) glue to an isomorphism
                                                              ∼
                                        Φ𝑎,𝑘 : (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ) −
                                                           → 𝐵 (𝑋𝑎+𝑘 , 𝑓 𝑎+𝑘 ).
Proof. Horizontal integral translation preserves Im 𝑎, so 𝑎 + 𝑘 ∈ A. The three filling discs are pairwise
disjoint. The finite overlaps commute by (7.30), and the cusp overlap commutes by (7.40). The local maps
therefore glue. Their chartwise inverses are
                                                                                   −1
                                   𝑇−𝑏𝑎,𝑘 ,      𝑇−𝑐1,𝑎,𝑘 ,       𝑇−𝑐2,𝑎,𝑘 ,     𝑢 𝑎,𝑘 ,
and the same overlap identities show that these inverses glue and compose to the identity.
   The two parameters lie in a common relatively compact disc in A; the explicit half-plane construction in the
proof of Corollary 2.28 applies to this pair. The comparison maps constructed in the proof of Proposition 2.25
92                          YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


(Appendix B) restrict the same core, finite, and cusp formulae and satisfy their cocycle identity. Thus the
glued map induces the asserted isomorphism of the fixed intrinsic pairs, independently of those two auxiliary
choices.                                                                                                   □
Remark 7.9. Changing a logarithm used to specify ℓ or 𝜅 𝑗 adds an integer and hence changes the corresponding
translation by the period 𝑘𝑛u2 . Changing the logarithm used for 𝜅0 has the same effect before exponentiation
and therefore leaves 𝑢 𝑎,𝑘 unchanged.
   Any other pair of global roots has the form 𝐹 ′ = 𝛼𝐹, 𝐺 ′ = 𝛽𝐺, with 𝛼 ∈ 𝜇3 and 𝛽 ∈ 𝜇4 . It changes ℓ by a
              1
constant 𝑐 ∈ 12  Z/Z and changes the global isomorphism by
                                              Φ′𝑎,𝑘 = 𝑇𝑘𝑐u2 ◦ Φ𝑎,𝑘 .
Thus the existence of even-shift isomorphisms and the resulting orbit relation are independent of the global
roots, but an individual map is not canonical before a root normalization is chosen. No independence is
claimed under changing the fixed finite uniformizers, cusp splitting, or fan marking.
Proof of the pair-equivalence assertion in Theorem 1.4. The implication from a pair isomorphism to an even
parameter difference is Proposition 7.5. The converse is Proposition 7.8. The orbit-curve assertion is proved
in Corollary 7.15.                                                                                          □
7.3. Automorphisms, orbit classes, and deformation consequences. In the automorphism argument, the
global roots 𝐹, 𝐺, the finite uniformizers, the cusp splitting, and the fan marking are fixed. The comparison
theorem of Proposition 2.25 applies to the cusp cutoff and auxiliary parameter discs.
    For 𝑐 ∈ C/Z, let 𝑇𝑐 denote translation by 𝑐u2 . It is an automorphism of every distinguished pair. Indeed,
it is an integral-period translation on the core when 𝑐 changes by an integer; it commutes with the finite affine
actions because 𝐴 𝑗 e4 = e4 ; and at the cusp it is multiplication by the unit (1, e2 𝜋i𝑐 ). Hence these translations
give a vertical action
                                              C/Z ≃ C∗ ↷ (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ).                                          (7.41)
Its effectivity is exactly the kernel calculation in Lemma 6.17. By Theorem 1.3, this is the connected
automorphism group.
    We now show that there are no further components. The proof has two separate cusp steps. First, a
transverse-disc lifting obstruction kills the integral class of a core translation. Second, a zero-class translation
is shown to possess additive logarithms across all three fillings.
Lemma 7.10. Every element of Aut 𝐵 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ) has identity linear part on the smooth torus fibres. Its

together with (7.12), gives 3 | (1 − 𝜀) and hence 𝜀 = 1. The pair automorphism is therefore a fibrewise
translation on the smooth core.

Lemma 7.12. If a pair automorphism has zero translation class, then its translation coefficient extends to a
section
                                          𝑏 ∈ 𝐻 0 (B, 𝐸).
Proof. Choose the additive lift on the core so that its cocycle vanishes:
                                                𝑏(𝑔𝑧) = 𝑅𝑔 (𝑧)𝑏(𝑧).                                          (7.50)
At a finite point, functoriality of the normalization after root base change from Lemma 7.3 lifts the
automorphism to the smooth torus family on the root disc. Its linear part is the identity on the punctured disc
and hence everywhere by the identity theorem. It is therefore translation by a holomorphic torus section. The
root disc is simply connected, so this section has a holomorphic additive lift. On a punctured overlap, that
lift and 𝑏 differ by one fixed integral period. Adjusting by this period makes the two lifts agree and extends
(7.50) through both finite root charts.
    At the cusp, 𝑅𝑔0 = 𝐼2 and the zero cocycle give 𝑏(𝑠 − 1) = 𝑏(𝑠). Hence 𝑏 descends to a single-valued
holomorphic map on the punctured 𝑞-disc, and
                                          𝑢(𝑞) = exp(2𝜋i𝑏(𝑞)) ∈ (C∗ ) 2                                      (7.51)
has a global logarithm there. In particular, each component of 𝑢 has winding number zero.
   Because the original quotient automorphism extends across the central fibre, its composite with the covering
map 𝑝 𝑎 has holomorphic local lifts near each point of that fibre. Shrink their source neighbourhoods to toric
polydiscs. The complement of 𝑞 = 0 in each such polydisc is connected, and on it a local lift and multiplication
by 𝑢(𝑞) are lifts of the same quotient map. They therefore differ by one fixed deck transformation; adjust
the lift by its inverse. On every intersection the adjusted lifts agree on the punctured part, because both
equal multiplication by 𝑢(𝑞). That part is dense in each connected component of the intersection, so the
identity theorem makes the holomorphic lifts agree throughout. They glue to a lift near 𝑞 = 0 that restricts to
multiplication by 𝑢(𝑞) on the dense torus.
   For every toric character 𝜒 𝛼 , the identity on the dense torus gives
                                                 e∗ 𝜒 𝛼
                                                 Φ
                                                        = 𝑢(𝑞) 𝛼 .                                        (7.52)
                                                   𝜒𝛼
The left-hand side is meromorphic across the toric boundary. Choose a general smooth point of a central toric
component at which 𝑞 is a transverse coordinate, and restrict (7.52) to a small transverse 𝑞-disc through that
point. Thus 𝑢(𝑞) 𝛼 is a one-variable meromorphic germ at 𝑞 = 0. Applying this observation to a basis of the
character lattice shows that both components of 𝑢 are meromorphic at the origin. Write
                                𝑢 𝑖 (𝑞) = 𝑞 𝑑𝑖 𝑣 𝑖 (𝑞),   𝑑𝑖 ∈ Z,      𝑣 𝑖 (0) ≠ 0.

holomorphic unit. A logarithm on the full disc differs from the original 𝑏 𝑖 on the punctured disc by a constant
integer; after that period is adjusted, 𝑏 extends in the cusp logarithmic frame.

  The core, finite, and cusp lifts now have exactly the transition matrices used to specify the vertical bundle

Proposition 7.13. For every 𝑎 ∈ A,
                                   Aut 𝐵 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ) = {𝑇𝑐 : 𝑐 ∈ C}/Z ≃ C∗ .
Proof. By Lemmas 7.10 and 7.11, every pair automorphism has zero translation class. By Lemma 7.12, it is

Lemma 6.17 identifies precisely the parameters differing by Z.                                       □
Theorem 7.14. For every 𝑎 ∈ A,
                                       Aut(𝑋𝑎 ) = AutP1 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ) ≃ C∗ .
Proof. The intrinsic characterization in Theorem 7.1 makes every bare automorphism strict over 𝐵. Hence
                                        Aut(𝑋𝑎 ) = Aut 𝐵 (𝑋𝑎 , 𝑓 𝑎 ) ≃ C∗
by Proposition 7.13. The right-hand side is connected and agrees with the identity component already
identified in Theorem 1.3.                                                                         □
   The intrinsic fibration and the pair-equivalence criterion also settle biholomorphism of the unmarked
threefolds.
Proof of the bare-equivalence assertion in Theorem 1.4. If 𝑎 ′ − 𝑎 ∈ 2Z, the pair isomorphism supplied by
Theorem 1.4 is in particular a biholomorphism 𝑋𝑎 ≃ 𝑋𝑎′ . Conversely, every biholomorphism 𝑋𝑎 ≃ 𝑋𝑎′
is an isomorphism of pairs over 𝐵 by Theorem 7.1. The necessity assertion in Theorem 1.4 then gives
𝑎 ′ − 𝑎 ∈ 2Z.                                                                                           □

  By Theorem 1.4 and Corollary 7.15, this curve is the analytic orbit curve of both pair-isomorphism and
bare-biholomorphism classes in the present family.
  We combine the relative Oka theorem with the complete local deformation calculation. The single-fibre
geometry is available at every admissible parameter by Theorem 3.11.
  By Theorem 1.1, the two distinguished complements and the compact member are Oka at every admissible
parameter, and every relative projection over a relatively compact parameter disc is an Oka map.
Corollary 7.16. Let 𝑝 : Y → 𝑆 be a proper holomorphic submersion over a complex manifold, with a
marked fibre Y𝑠0 ≃ 𝑋𝑎 for some 𝑎 ∈ A. There is a neighbourhood 𝑆 ′ ∋ 𝑠0 such that 𝑝 : 𝑝 −1 (𝑆 ′ ) → 𝑆 ′ is an
Oka map.
96                           YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


Proof. By Theorem 1.1, choose a small parameter disc Δ ⋐ A about 𝑎 on which 𝜋Δ is Oka. By Theorem 1.3,
after further shrinking and local reparameterization this is a miniversal deformation of 𝑋𝑎 . The given proper
holomorphic submersion 𝑝 : Y → 𝑆 is a deformation of its marked compact central fibre. Local completeness
gives, after shrinking 𝑆 to a neighbourhood 𝑆 ′ of 𝑠0 , a holomorphic map 𝜑 : 𝑆 ′ → Δ, with 𝜑(𝑠0 ) = 𝑎, and an
isomorphism of families
                                              Y| 𝑆 ′ ≃ 𝑆 ′ ×Δ 𝔛 Δ .
The classifying map is not required to be unique. By Forstnerič [23, Theorem 4.1 and the following discussion,
p. 803], the pullback of an Oka map by a holomorphic map is Oka. Hence 𝑝 : Y| 𝑆 ′ → 𝑆 ′ is an Oka map. □
Corollary 7.17. Every parameter has a disc neighbourhood Δ on which 𝜋Δ is an Oka map and distinct
parameters give nonbiholomorphic fibres. The family on Δ is miniversal after shrinking and reparameterization.
Proof. Choose Δ ⋐ A of diameter less than 2. If 𝑎, 𝑎 ′ ∈ Δ and 𝑋𝑎 ≃ 𝑋𝑎′ , the exact-period theorem
gives 𝑎 ′ − 𝑎 ∈ 2Z. The diameter bound forces 𝑎 ′ = 𝑎. The Oka and miniversality assertions follow from
Theorems 1.1 and 1.3.                                                                                 □

   The product covers in Section 3 are compatible with these distinct compact deformation classes. They
remove the parameter from the lattice subgroup ker 𝛾; the omitted generator still acts by a parameter-dependent
deck map. Passing to its quotient recovers the varying compact geometry. The orbit coordinate 𝑤 = exp(−𝜋𝑖𝑎)
identifies exactly the even-integer repetitions, and the strictified groupoid in Proposition C.4 also records the
constant isotropy C∗ . These are statements about the explicitly constructed family, without an identification
of an ambient analytic moduli component.

                                    Appendix A. Analytic quotient tools
     This section records the analytic tools in the forms used in the proofs.
Definition A.1. A reduced complex space is a complex analytic space whose structure sheaf has no nonzero
nilpotent sections. A reduced complex space 𝑌 is normal if its normalization map 𝜈 : 𝑌 𝜈 → 𝑌 is an
isomorphism. Equivalently, for every 𝑦 ∈ 𝑌 , the local ring O𝑌 ,𝑦 is integrally closed in its total ring of fractions.
This definition applies whether or not 𝑌 is globally irreducible.
Definition A.2. A continuous map 𝑝 : 𝑀 → 𝑆 between Hausdorff spaces is proper if 𝑝 −1 (𝐾) is compact for
every compact subset 𝐾 ⊂ 𝑆. A holomorphic map of complex spaces is called proper when its underlying
continuous map is proper.
Definition A.3. A holomorphic map 𝑝 : 𝑌 → 𝑍 of complex spaces is finite if it is proper and every fibre has
finite underlying set.
  For such a map, the Finite Mapping Theorem implies that 𝑝 ∗ O𝑌 is a coherent O𝑍 -module; see Grauert–
Remmert [7, Chapter 3, Section 1.3, pp. 64–65].
Definition A.4. Let a discrete group 𝐺 act on a Hausdorff space 𝑀. The action is properly discontinuous if,
for every pair of compact subsets 𝐾1 , 𝐾2 ⊂ 𝑀, the set
                                             {𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 : 𝑔𝐾1 ∩ 𝐾2 ≠ ∅}
is finite. The action is free if no nonidentity element fixes a point.
Proposition A.5. Let 𝑀 be a Hausdorff second-countable complex manifold, and let a countable discrete
group 𝐺 act freely and properly discontinuously on 𝑀 by biholomorphisms. Then the orbit space 𝑀/𝐺
has a unique structure of a Hausdorff second-countable complex manifold for which the quotient map
𝑞 : 𝑀 → 𝑀/𝐺 is a local biholomorphism. If 𝑝 : 𝑀 → 𝑆 is a 𝐺-invariant holomorphic map to a complex
manifold 𝑆, then it descends uniquely to a holomorphic map 𝑝¯ : 𝑀/𝐺 → 𝑆. If 𝑝 is a submersion, then 𝑝¯ is a
submersion.
Proof. The compact-set condition above is properness of the discrete action. Lee’s holomorphic quotient
theorem [8, Theorem 1.16] gives the complex manifold structure and the local biholomorphism 𝑞. The
invariant map descends in quotient charts. Since 𝑑𝑝 = 𝑑 𝑝¯ ◦ 𝑑𝑞 and 𝑑𝑞 is an isomorphism, a submersion 𝑝
descends to a submersion.                                                                              □
                  OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                            97


Proposition A.6. Let a discrete group 𝐺 act properly discontinuously on a Hausdorff space 𝑀, let 𝐻 ≤ 𝐺,
and let 𝑉 ⊂ 𝑀 be an open 𝐻-invariant subset. Assume
                                    𝑔𝑉 ∩ 𝑉 = ∅        for every 𝑔 ∈ 𝐺 \ 𝐻.                                 (A.1)
Then the orbit map 𝑀 → 𝑀/𝐺 induces a homeomorphism
                                                      
                                               𝑉/𝐻 −−→ 𝑞 𝐺 (𝑉)
onto an open subset of 𝑀/𝐺. If 𝑀 is a complex manifold, both actions are by biholomorphisms, and the
actions on 𝑀 and 𝑉 are free, then this homeomorphism is biholomorphic for the quotient complex structures
furnished by Proposition A.5.
Proof. The orbit maps are open, and condition (A.1) says that two points of 𝑉 lie in the same 𝐺-orbit precisely
when they lie in the same 𝐻-orbit. Thus the induced map 𝑉/𝐻 → 𝑞 𝐺 (𝑉) is an open continuous bijection.
Under the complex hypotheses it is locally the identity in the quotient charts of Proposition A.5, hence
biholomorphic.                                                                                               □
Lemma A.7. Let 𝐺 act on a Hausdorff space 𝑀, let 𝑞 : 𝑀 → 𝑀/𝐺 be the quotient map, and let 𝑝 : 𝑀 → 𝑆
be a 𝐺-invariant continuous map. Assume that 𝑀/𝐺 is Hausdorff. Suppose that for every compact 𝐾 ⊂ 𝑆
there exists a compact set 𝐶𝐾 ⊂ 𝑝 −1 (𝐾) meeting every 𝐺-orbit contained in 𝑝 −1 (𝐾). Then the descended
map 𝑝¯ : 𝑀/𝐺 → 𝑆 is proper.
Proof. For compact 𝐾 ⊂ 𝑆, every orbit over 𝐾 meets 𝐶𝐾 , so
                                               𝑝¯ −1 (𝐾) = 𝑞(𝐶𝐾 ).
The continuous image of a compact set is compact. Hence 𝑝¯ −1 (𝐾) is compact.                                  □
   A holomorphic map between reduced irreducible complex spaces is bimeromorphic if it restricts to a
biholomorphism outside proper closed analytic subsets of source and target.
Theorem A.8. Let 𝐹 : 𝑌 → 𝑍 be a proper holomorphic map of complex analytic spaces, and let 𝐴 ⊂ 𝑌 be a
closed analytic subset. Then 𝐹 ( 𝐴) ⊂ 𝑍 is a closed analytic subset. In particular, if 𝑌 is compact, the image
of any holomorphic map 𝑌 → 𝑍 is a compact analytic subset of 𝑍.
  For the proper mapping theorem, applied to the restriction 𝐹 | 𝐴, see Grauert–Remmert [7, Chapter 10,
Section 6.1, p. 213].
Theorem A.9. Let 𝑍 be a reduced complex space. There exist a normal complex space 𝑍 𝜈 and a finite
surjective holomorphic map 𝜈 𝑍 : 𝑍 𝜈 → 𝑍 with the following properties.
   (i) Over the normal locus of 𝑍, the map 𝜈 𝑍 is a biholomorphism.
  (ii) Locally, if 𝑍1 , . . . , 𝑍𝑟 are the irreducible branches of 𝑍, then 𝜈 𝑍−1 (𝑈) is the disjoint union of the
       normalizations of those branches.
 (iii) If 𝑌 is normal and 𝑝 : 𝑌 → 𝑍 is holomorphic with no irreducible component of 𝑌 mapped into the
       nonnormal locus of 𝑍, then 𝑝 factors uniquely through 𝜈 𝑍 .
The pair (𝑍 𝜈 , 𝜈 𝑍 ) is unique up to a unique biholomorphism over 𝑍.
  For existence, the local branch description, and the universal property, see Grauert–Remmert [7, Chapter 7,
Section 2.1, p. 133; Chapter 8, Sections 3.3 and 4.1–4.3, pp. 161 and 163–165].
Theorem A.10. Let 𝑍 be a reduced irreducible complex space, and let 𝜈 𝑍 : 𝑍 𝜈 → 𝑍 be its normalization.
Let 𝑌 be a normal irreducible complex space, and let 𝑝 : 𝑌 → 𝑍 be a finite surjective bimeromorphic
holomorphic map. Then there exists a unique biholomorphism 𝜃 : 𝑌 → 𝑍 𝜈 satisfying
                                                  𝜈 𝑍 ◦ 𝜃 = 𝑝.
Equivalently, every finite surjective bimeromorphic map from a normal irreducible complex space to 𝑍 is the
normalization map, up to a unique isomorphism over 𝑍.
  For this criterion, see Grauert–Remmert [7, Chapter 8, Sections 3.3 and 4.2, pp. 161 and 164; Chapter 9,
Section 3.3, pp. 179–180].
98                           YUN-HENG DU, BIN GUO, YUTA KUSAKABE, AND SONG-YAN XIE


Theorem A.11. Let 𝑝 : 𝑀 → 𝑆 be a surjective proper 𝐶 ∞ submersion between smooth paracompact
manifolds. Then every point of 𝑆 has an open neighbourhood 𝑉 and a diffeomorphism
                                                   𝑝 −1 (𝑉)  𝐹 × 𝑉
over 𝑉, where 𝐹 is a smooth manifold. Thus 𝑝 is a locally trivial smooth fibre bundle, and all fibres over one
connected component of 𝑆 are diffeomorphic.
  An early compact-fibre form of the theorem, together with the horizontal-lift argument, appears in
Ehresmann [5, pp. 154–155]; the stated proper-map formulation is its standard modern form.
Lemma A.12. Let 𝑉/Λ and 𝑉 ′ /Λ′ be compact complex tori. Every holomorphic map
                                                 𝜑 : 𝑉/Λ −→ 𝑉 ′ /Λ′
has a unique expression
                                                 𝜑( [𝑧]) = [𝐿𝑧 + 𝑏],
where 𝐿 : 𝑉 → 𝑉 ′ is complex linear, 𝐿 (Λ) ⊂ Λ′ , and 𝑏 ∈ 𝑉 ′ is defined modulo Λ′ . If 𝜑(0) = 0, then 𝑏 = 0
modulo Λ′ , so 𝜑 is a homomorphism of complex Lie groups.
     This affine description of maps between complex tori is standard [9, Chapter 1, §2].
Lemma A.13. Let 𝑓 : 𝑋 → 𝑆 be a continuous map between Hausdorff spaces. Suppose every point of 𝑆 has
an open neighbourhood 𝑈 such that 𝑓 −1 (𝑈) → 𝑈 is proper. Then 𝑓 is proper.
Proof. Let 𝐾 ⊂ 𝑆 be compact. Choose finitely many properness neighbourhoods 𝑈1 , . . . , 𝑈 𝑁 covering 𝐾.
The compact Hausdorff space 𝐾 is normal. For each 𝑥 ∈ 𝐾, choose 𝑖(𝑥) and an open neighbourhood 𝑉𝑥 in 𝐾
with 𝑥 ∈ 𝑉𝑥 ⊂ 𝑉 𝑥 ⊂ 𝑈𝑖 ( 𝑥 ) ∩ 𝐾. Finitely many 𝑉𝑥1 , . . . , 𝑉𝑥𝑟 cover 𝐾. Each 𝐹 𝑗 := 𝑉 𝑥 𝑗 is compact and lies in
𝑈𝑖 ( 𝑥 𝑗 ) , so 𝑓 −1 (𝐹 𝑗 ) is compact by properness over that neighbourhood. Their finite union is 𝑓 −1 (𝐾), proving
properness.                                                                                                         □

                                 Appendix B. Toric and gluing verifications
     This section gives the normalization, quotient, and gluing arguments used in Section 2.
     The central divisor is normalized by applying these toric charts to its individual components.
Proof of Lemma 2.19. In a maximal toric chart one has 𝑞 = 𝑧0 𝑧 1 𝑧 2 . At a point where exactly 𝑟 ∈ {1, 2, 3}
components meet, the germ of 𝐷𝑌 is
                                           {𝑧0 · · · 𝑧𝑟 −1 = 0} ⊂ C3 .
It is reduced. Its irreducible branches are the coordinate hyperplanes 𝑧𝑖 = 0, each of which is smooth and
therefore normal. By Theorem A.9, the normalization of the local ring
                                             C{𝑧 0 , 𝑧1 , 𝑧2 }/(𝑧 0 · · · 𝑧𝑟 −1 )
is the direct sum of the 𝑟 branch rings obtained by setting one 𝑧 𝑖 equal to zero. These local branch
normalizations agree on overlaps because they are the restrictions of the globally defined components 𝐸 𝑣 .
Every point has an affine toric neighbourhood attached to one cone, and only the finitely many rays of that
cone give components through the point. Thus the local normalization has finitely many branches at every
point, and the local branch normalizations glue to the disjoint union (2.46).                            □
     The lattice action lifts to this normalization as follows.
Proof of Proposition 2.20. The restriction of Ψ𝜆 to 𝐷𝑌 carries the component 𝐸 𝑣 biholomorphically onto
𝐸 𝑣+𝐵0 𝜆 . On the normalization this gives the lift 𝐸 𝑣 → 𝐸 𝑣+𝐵0 𝜆 ; uniqueness of normalization lifts gives its
group law. Since 𝐵0 ∈ GL(2, Z), the action on component indices is simply transitive, and hence the lifted
action is free. A compact subset of the disjoint union 𝐷 e𝑌 = Ý𝑣 𝐸 𝑣 meets only finitely many components.
For two compact subsets, only the finitely many translations between those component indices can carry
one to meet the other. Thus the action is properly discontinuous and has the analytic quotient furnished by
Proposition A.5.
   Every orbit meets 𝐸 0 exactly once. Explicitly, on 𝐸 𝑣 the unique map to 𝐸 0 is Ψ e −1 ; these maps are
                                                                                      −𝐵0 𝑣,𝑎
holomorphic and constant on orbits by the group law. They descend to a holomorphic inverse to the quotient
                                  e𝑌 /Z2 ≃ 𝐸 0 biholomorphically.
map restricted to 𝐸 0 . Therefore 𝐷                                                                           □
     We next verify that the four local pieces glue to a Hausdorff space.

Proof of Lemma 2.23. Write
                                              𝐶 = JΔ ,        𝑁 𝑗 = N 𝑗,Δ .
Let
                                      𝑂 𝐶𝑗 = 𝐶 | 𝐷 ∗𝑗 ×Δ ,        𝑂 𝑁𝑗 = 𝑁 𝑗 | 𝐷 ∗𝑗 ×Δ .
For uniform notation, let 𝜙 𝑗 : 𝑂 𝐶𝑗 → 𝑂 𝑁𝑗 be 𝐺 0 when 𝑗 = 0 and 𝐺 −1𝑗 when 𝑗 = 1, 2. The gluing relation is
generated by
                                           𝑥 ∼ 𝜙 𝑗 (𝑥)   (𝑥 ∈ 𝑂 𝐶𝑗 ).
Because the discs 𝐷 0 , 𝐷 1 , 𝐷 2 are pairwise disjoint, the three open sets 𝑂 𝐶𝑗 are pairwise disjoint. No point of
one filling piece is identified with a point of another filling piece. It follows directly that every equivalence
class is either a singleton or a two-point set
                                                     {𝑥, 𝜙 𝑗 (𝑥)}
for a unique index 𝑗 and a unique 𝑥 ∈ 𝑂 𝑗 . In particular, an equivalence class contains at most one point from
                                           𝐶

each individual piece. Hence the canonical map from every piece to the glued quotient is injective.
   The image of each piece is open. For example, the saturation of the core is
                                              𝐶 ⊔ 𝑂 0𝑁 ⊔ 𝑂 1𝑁 ⊔ 𝑂 2𝑁 ,
which is open in the disjoint union, and the saturation of 𝑁 𝑗 is 𝑁 𝑗 ⊔ 𝑂 𝐶𝑗 . The canonical injection of a piece
is therefore a homeomorphism onto an open subset. Since the glued space is covered by four open subsets
homeomorphic to second-countable spaces, the union of four countable bases is a countable basis for the
glued space. The complex charts on the pieces have holomorphic transition functions because the only
cross-piece transitions are the biholomorphisms 𝜙 𝑗 and 𝜙 −1    𝑗 . Thus they determine a unique complex-manifold
structure once Hausdorffness has been proved.
    Let 𝑥 ≠ 𝑦 be points of the glued space. The local maps to 𝐵 × Δ agree on the overlaps, so they determine a
continuous map 𝔣. If 𝔣(𝑥) ≠ 𝔣(𝑦), choose disjoint neighbourhoods of their images in the Hausdorff base and
take inverse images.
    Suppose 𝔣(𝑥) = 𝔣(𝑦) = (𝑏, 𝑎). If 𝑏 ∈ 𝐷 𝑗 , represent both points in the filling piece 𝑁 𝑗 ; when 𝑏 ∈ 𝐷 ∗𝑗 this
is possible using 𝜙 𝑗 . If 𝑏 lies outside the three filling discs, represent both points in the core 𝐶. Thus there is
one Hausdorff piece 𝑀 containing distinct representatives e           𝑦 of 𝑥, 𝑦.

                  Appendix C. Integral calculations and the constructed groupoid
   The rational centralizer calculation follows from a sequence of explicit scalar deductions. Let
                      𝐶1 = 𝑄 𝐴1 − 𝐴1 𝑄,            𝐶2 = 𝑄 𝐴2 − 𝐴2 𝑄,                 𝑄 = (𝑞 𝑟 𝑠 )1≤𝑟 ,𝑠≤4 .
Assume 𝐶1 = 𝐶2 = 0.
  First use the entries
                                         (𝐶1 )11 = 6𝑞 12 − 6𝑞 13 − 2𝑞 14 = 0,                                  (C.1)

                                         (𝐶2 )11 = −6𝑞 13 + 3𝑞 14 = 0.                                         (C.3)
Equation (C.3) gives 𝑞 14 = 2𝑞 13 . Substitution into (C.2) gives 𝑞 12 = 𝑞 13 . Substitution of both relations into
(C.1) gives −4𝑞 13 = 0. Hence
                                              𝑞 12 = 𝑞 13 = 𝑞 14 = 0.                                        (C.4)
   Next use

Vertical translations commute with the strict shifts, and (C.23) is the composition law. For each fixed 𝑘, the
core, finite, and cusp formulae depend jointly holomorphically on 𝑎 on every common parameter disc; the
comparison cocycle preserves these maps on overlaps. The arrows therefore form an analytic family, and we
obtain the constructed analytic action groupoid
                                              (C∗ × 2Z) ⋉ A ⇒ A.                                              (C.25)
Proposition C.4. After the fixed analytic choices and quadratic strictification, the constructed action groupoid
has quotient
                                       [A/(C∗ × 2Z)] ≃ 𝐷 ∗𝑀0 × 𝐵C∗ .
Its arrows exhaust the biholomorphisms between every pair of geometric fibres.
Proof. The C∗ -factor acts trivially on the parameter and commutes with the 2Z-factor, giving the first product
decomposition. The 2Z-action is free and properly discontinuous, so its analytic quotient stack is represented
by the quotient curve. The orbit-coordinate calculation above therefore gives
                               [A/(C∗ × 2Z)] ≃ [A/2Z] × 𝐵C∗ ≃ 𝐷 ∗𝑀0 × 𝐵C∗ .                                   (C.26)
  It remains to prove the assertion about geometric arrows. If Ψ : 𝑋𝑎 → 𝑋𝑎′ is a biholomorphism, then
Theorem 1.4 gives 𝑎 ′ = 𝑎 + 𝑘 for some 𝑘 ∈ 2Z. The composite
                                         Ψ◦Φ e −1 ∈ Aut(𝑋𝑎+𝑘 )
                                                     𝑎,𝑘
is 𝑇𝑐 for a unique 𝑐 ∈ C/Z by Theorem 7.14. Hence
                                                  Ψ = 𝑇𝑐 ◦ Φ
                                                           e 𝑎,𝑘 ,
which is exactly an arrow of (C.24). The converse is part of the construction.                                     □
  The quotient in (C.26) is the quotient of the explicit action in (C.25); the C∗ factor records its isotropy on
geometric fibres.

                     Appendix D. The source complex structure on the six-sphere
   The complex structure used throughout this paper originates in Alpöge’s construction [2, Construction 6.1
and Theorem 8.1]. Its underlying idea is to complete a family of complex two-tori over a three-punctured
projective line in three different ways. The finite monodromies at two punctures lead to smooth multiple
fibres; the unipotent monodromy at the third leads to a reduced, nonnormal toric fibre. The choices of the
three completions also determine the integral topology of the total space. We describe how these parts fit
together, using the notation of Section 2.
   Take 𝐵 = P1𝑡 with 𝑝 1 = 0, 𝑝 2 = 1, 𝑝 0 = ∞, and 𝐵◦ = 𝐵 \ {𝑝 0 , 𝑝 1 , 𝑝 2 }. Alpöge [2, Proposition 2.11] uses
the hyperbolic orbifold of signature (0; 3, 4, ∞) on 𝐵. Its upper-half-plane uniformization 𝑈 has group
                                Γ = ⟨𝑔1 , 𝑔2 | 𝑔13 = 𝑔24 = 1⟩,       𝑔0 = (𝑔1 𝑔2 ) −1 ,
where 𝑔 𝑗 fixes a point over 𝑝 𝑗 and 𝑔0 is the primitive parabolic element at 𝑝 0 . Removing the two finite-stabilizer
orbits gives a covering 𝑈 ◦ → 𝐵◦ . Thus the base simultaneously supplies an order-three point, an order-four
point, and a cusp; each has a distinct local completion of the torus family.
    The integral lattice Λ = Z⟨b   𝛾, b
                                      𝑢, 𝑤  𝛿⟩ will be the first homology of a smooth fibre. The representation
                                         b, b

                           3      4

𝛾 : Λ → Z is fixed by both finite monodromies; their coinvariants leave precisely the circle measured by 𝛾.

   Alpöge [2, Theorem 3.4] realizes this monodromy by holomorphic period functions 𝜏, 𝜇, 𝛽 on 𝑈. The first

conjugates this affine action to the original monodromy action. Changing the branch of log 𝑠 𝑗 adds an integral
period, so the identification with 𝐽𝑎 over the punctured disc is single-valued.
   The cusp requires a different operation. On a simply connected horodisc above 𝑝 0 , choose a logarithmic
coordinate 𝑠 with 𝑞 = 𝑒 2 𝜋i𝑠 = 1/𝑡 and 𝑠(𝑔0 𝑧) = 𝑠(𝑧) − 1. The period block 𝑍 𝑎 formed by the first two
columns of Ω𝑎 has the form
                                   𝑍 𝑎 = 𝑠𝐵0 + 𝐶𝑎 (𝑞),          𝐶𝑎 ∈ O (𝐷 0 , 𝑀2 (C)),
because 𝜏 − 𝑠, 𝜇, and 𝛽0 + 𝑎 + 𝜏 extend across 𝑞 = 0. Quotienting first by the last two periods, those of Λtor ,
turns the fibre coordinates into (C∗ ) 2 . For 𝜆¯ ∈ Λ, the remaining period acts there by multiplication by
                                                    ¯
                                               𝑞 𝐵0 𝜆 exp 2𝜋i𝐶𝑎 (𝑞) 𝜆¯ .
                                                                      

The singular factor is thus an integral monomial in 𝑞, while the second factor is a holomorphic unit. This is
the passage from the analytic period family to Alpöge’s toric filling [2, Lemma 4.2 and Theorem 4.5].
    More explicitly, triangulate R2 periodically by the two unimodular triangles in each unit square and cone
the triangles from the origin in R2 ⊕ R at height one. The resulting countable fan is smooth. Its toric threefold
has a holomorphic height character 𝑞, and on each maximal affine chart this map reads 𝑞 = 𝑧0 𝑧 1 𝑧 2 . Hence
the central divisor upstairs is reduced with simple normal crossings. Translation of the fan by 𝐵0 𝜆,  ¯ together
with the holomorphic unit in the preceding display, extends the residual Λ-action over 𝑞 = 0. Alpöge proves
that, on a sufficiently small cusp disc, this action is free and properly discontinuous and its quotient is proper
over the disc. Since 𝐵0 Λ = Z2 , all vertices of the periodic fan lie in one orbit. The quotient central fibre 𝑊
is therefore irreducible and nonnormal, although it has normal-crossing singularities. Its normalization is
the degree-six del Pezzo surface: the six boundary curves form a hexagon, and opposite sides are identified
in 𝑊, producing three double curves and two triple points [2, Proposition 4.6]. Exponentiating the fibre
coordinates identifies the toric quotient over 𝑞 ≠ 0 with the original family 𝐽𝑎 over the punctured cusp disc [2,
Proposition 4.7].
    The three punctured identifications attach the two logarithmic fillings and the toric cusp filling to the
same smooth torus family. Alpöge [2, Construction 6.1 and Theorem 6.2] shows that the resulting space is a
                      OKA GEOMETRY AND DEFORMATIONS OF TORUS–FIBRED COMPLEX SIX-SPHERES                                              105


compact Hausdorff complex threefold 𝑋 A with a proper map to P1 and exactly the fibres described above.
The affine translations also enter its topology. In Alpöge’s notation the cusp gluing is untranslated, and the
character values are (ℓ0 , ℓ1 , ℓ2 ) = (0, 1, −1); consequently
                                                     12ℓ0 − 4ℓ1 − 3ℓ2 = −1.
Alpöge [2, Theorem 7.17, Corollary 7.29, and Theorem 8.1] proves that these choices make 𝑋 A simply
connected with the integral homology of 𝑆 6 , and then identifies its underlying smooth manifold with 𝑆 6 .
Transporting the complex atlas along that diffeomorphism gives the complex structure on the standard smooth
six-sphere.
   Our family uses precisely the monodromy matrices, finite twists, period normalization, and untranslated
cusp gluing just described. The constant 𝑎 varies the invariant period 𝛽0 + 𝑎 through the admissible half-plane;
the member 𝑎 = 𝑎 A is Alpöge’s compact threefold by Lemma 2.29. The jointly holomorphic fillings give
a proper submersion over that half-plane, so its fibres are all diffeomorphic to 𝑆 6 by Corollary 2.30. The
normalization of 𝑊 and its paired boundary curves enter the cusp resolution and cup-product calculation in
Section 6.

                                                Motivation and chronology
Motivation. For some time, S.-Y. Xie had an idea for proving that 𝑆 6 admits no complex structure. Suppose,
for contradiction, that 𝑆 6 admitted a complex structure. The resulting complex manifold would then be very
close to being Oka; using the scarcity of Oka manifolds, one would try to show that it was not Oka, thereby
obtaining a contradiction. This earlier idea did not succeed as originally formulated.
   During the 32nd International Conference on Finite or Infinite Dimensional Complex Analysis and
Applications, held in Quanzhou on 22–25 August 2026, Xie mentioned this earlier unsuccessful idea in a
conversation with Yuta Kusakabe. Kusakabe instead suggested considering whether 𝑆 6 —more precisely,
Alpöge’s complex six-sphere—is Oka. This suggestion initiated our collaboration.
Chronology and related work. By 27 August 2026, we had obtained the Oka result for the complex
six-sphere, and S.-Y. Xie informed F. Forstnerič of it by email on that date. Our other Oka results, including
the finite-point deletion and blow-up results, were also obtained by the end of August 2026. The present
manuscript was written before we learned of Chen’s preprint and was being polished when we became aware
of it. Chen’s preprint, submitted on 22 September 2026 [22], independently establishes the Oka property for
the admissible complex six-spheres. The results here additionally address the relative Oka map, finite-point
deletions and blow-ups, deformations, and exact biholomorphism classes. The finite-point arguments use
complete lifted centres, character-adapted coverings, and localization on the corrected incidence charts.
